U.S. state

For the river of the same name, see [Alabama River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alabama_River "Alabama River"). For other uses, see [Alabama (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alabama_\(disambiguation\) "Alabama (disambiguation)").

**Alabama** ([/ˌæləˈbæmə/](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:IPA/English "Help:IPA/English") [](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/5/54/LL-Q1860_%28eng%29-Flame%2C_not_lame-Alabama.wav/LL-Q1860_%28eng%29-Flame%2C_not_lame-Alabama.wav.mp3 "Play audio")[ⓘ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:LL-Q1860_\(eng\)-Flame,_not_lame-Alabama.wav "File:LL-Q1860 (eng)-Flame, not lame-Alabama.wav") [*AL-ə-BAM-ə*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:Pronunciation_respelling_key "Help:Pronunciation respelling key"))[\[9\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-10) is a [state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._state "U.S. state") in the [Southeastern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southeastern_United_States "Southeastern United States") region of the United States. It borders [Tennessee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tennessee "Tennessee") to the north, [Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgia_\(U.S._state\) "Georgia (U.S. state)") to the east, [Florida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florida "Florida") and the [Gulf of Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_of_Mexico "Gulf of Mexico") to the south, and [Mississippi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mississippi "Mississippi") to the west. Alabama is the [30th largest by area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_U.S._states_and_territories_by_area "List of U.S. states and territories by area"), and the [24th-most populous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_U.S._states_and_territories_by_population "List of U.S. states and territories by population") of the [50 U.S. states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_states_and_territories_of_the_United_States "List of states and territories of the United States").[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-11)[\[11\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-12)

Alabama is nicknamed the *[Yellowhammer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_flicker "Northern flicker") State*, after the [state bird](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_U.S._state_birds "List of U.S. state birds"). Alabama is also known as the "Heart of [Dixie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dixie "Dixie")" and the "Cotton State". The state has diverse geography, with the north dominated by the mountainous [Tennessee Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tennessee_Valley "Tennessee Valley") and the south by [Mobile Bay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mobile_Bay "Mobile Bay"), a historically significant port. Alabama's capital is [Montgomery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montgomery,_Alabama "Montgomery, Alabama"), and its largest city by population and area is [Huntsville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Huntsville,_Alabama "Huntsville, Alabama").[\[12\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-al.com-13) Its oldest city is [Mobile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mobile,_Alabama "Mobile, Alabama"), founded by [French colonists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_colonization_of_the_Americas "French colonization of the Americas") ([Alabama Creoles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alabama_Creole_people "Alabama Creole people")) in 1702 as the capital of [French Louisiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisiana_\(New_France\) "Louisiana (New France)").[\[13\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-pelican-14)[\[14\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-meltonmclaurin-15) [Greater Birmingham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birmingham_metropolitan_area,_Alabama "Birmingham metropolitan area, Alabama") is Alabama's largest metropolitan area and its economic center.[\[15\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-16) Politically, as part of the [Deep South](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deep_South "Deep South"), or "[Bible Belt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bible_Belt "Bible Belt")", Alabama is a predominantly [conservative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservatism_in_the_United_States "Conservatism in the United States") state, and is known for its [Southern culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_the_Southern_United_States "Culture of the Southern United States"). Within Alabama, [American football](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_football "American football"), particularly at the [college](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/College_football "College football") level, plays a major part of the state's culture.

Originally home to many native tribes, present-day Alabama was a Spanish territory beginning in the sixteenth century until the French acquired it in the early eighteenth century. The British won the territory in 1763 until losing it in the [American Revolutionary War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Revolutionary_War "American Revolutionary War"). Spain held Mobile as part of [Spanish West Florida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_West_Florida "Spanish West Florida") until 1813. In December 1819, Alabama was recognized as a state. During the antebellum period, Alabama was a major [producer of cotton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cotton_production_in_the_United_States "Cotton production in the United States"), and widely used [African American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_Americans "African Americans") [slave labor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavery_in_the_United_States "Slavery in the United States"). In 1861, the state seceded from the United States to become part of the [Confederate States of America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confederate_States_of_America "Confederate States of America"), with Montgomery acting as its first capital, and rejoined the Union in 1868. Following the [American Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Civil_War "American Civil War"), Alabama would suffer decades of economic hardship, in part due to agriculture and a few [cash crops](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cash_crops "Cash crops") being the main driver of the state's economy. Similar to other former slave states, Alabamian legislators employed [Jim Crow laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jim_Crow_laws "Jim Crow laws") from the late 19th century up until the 1960s. High-profile events such as the [Selma to Montgomery marches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Selma_to_Montgomery_marches "Selma to Montgomery marches") made the state a major focal point of the [civil rights movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_rights_movement "Civil rights movement") in the 1950s and 1960s.

During and after [World War II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_War_II "World War II"), Alabama grew as the state's [economy diversified](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_diversity "Economic diversity") with new industries. In 1960, the establishment of [NASA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NASA "NASA")'s [Marshall Space Flight Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marshall_Space_Flight_Center "Marshall Space Flight Center") in Huntsville helped boost Alabama's economic growth by developing a local [aerospace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aerospace "Aerospace") industry. Alabama's economy in the 21st century is based on automotive, finance, tourism, manufacturing, aerospace, mineral extraction, healthcare, education, retail, and technology.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-alaindustrial-17)

## Etymology

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alabama\&action=edit\&section=1 "Edit section: Etymology")]

The name of the [Alabama River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alabama_River "Alabama River") and state is derived from the [Alabama people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alabama_people "Alabama people"), a [Muskogean-speaking tribe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muskogean_languages "Muskogean languages") whose members lived just below the [confluence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confluence "Confluence") of the [Coosa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coosa_River "Coosa River") and [Tallapoosa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tallapoosa_River "Tallapoosa River") rivers on the upper reaches of the river.[\[17\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Read-18) In the [Alabama language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alabama_language "Alabama language"), the word for a person of Alabama lineage is *Albaamo* (or variously *Albaama* or *Albàamo* in different dialects; the plural form is *Albaamaha*).[\[18\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-19) The word's spelling varies significantly among historical sources.[\[19\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-ADAH1-20) The first usage appears in three accounts of the [Hernando de Soto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hernando_de_Soto "Hernando de Soto") expedition of 1540: [Garcilaso de la Vega](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inca_Garcilaso_de_la_Vega "Inca Garcilaso de la Vega") used *Alibamo*, while the Knight of Elvas and Rodrigo Ranjel wrote *Alibamu* and *Limamu*, respectively, in [transliterations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transliteration "Transliteration") of the term.[\[19\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-ADAH1-20)

As early as 1702, the French called the tribe the *Alibamon*, with French maps identifying the river as *Rivière des Alibamons*.[\[17\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Read-18) Other spellings of the name have included *Alibamu*, *Alabamo*, *Albama*, *Alebamon*, *Alibama*, *Alibamou*, *Alabamu*, and *Allibamou*.[\[19\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-ADAH1-20)[\[20\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Wills-21)[\[21\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Griffith-22) The use of state names derived from [Native American languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_languages_of_the_Americas "Indigenous languages of the Americas") is common in the U.S. An estimated 26 states have names of Native American origin.[\[22\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Weiss-23)

Sources disagree on the word's meaning. Some scholars suggest the word comes from the Choctaw *alba* (meaning 'plants' or 'weeds') and *amo* (meaning 'to cut', 'to trim', or 'to gather').[\[19\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-ADAH1-20)[\[23\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Rogers-24)[\[24\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Swanton1-25) The meaning may have been 'clearers of the thicket'[\[23\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Rogers-24) or 'herb gatherers',[\[24\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Swanton1-25)[\[25\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Swanton2-26) referring to clearing land for cultivation[\[20\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Wills-21) or collecting medicinal plants.[\[25\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Swanton2-26) The state has numerous [place names of Native American origin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_place_names_of_Native_American_origin_in_Alabama "List of place names of Native American origin in Alabama").[\[26\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-27)[\[27\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-28)

An 1842 article in the *Jacksonville Republican* proposed it meant 'Here We Rest'.[\[19\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-ADAH1-20) This notion was popularized in the 1850s through the writings of [Alexander Beaufort Meek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_Beaufort_Meek "Alexander Beaufort Meek").[\[19\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-ADAH1-20) Experts in the Muskogean languages have not found any evidence to support such a translation.[\[17\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Read-18)[\[19\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-ADAH1-20)

## History

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alabama\&action=edit\&section=2 "Edit section: History")]

Main article: [History of Alabama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Alabama "History of Alabama")

### Pre-European settlement

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alabama\&action=edit\&section=3 "Edit section: Pre-European settlement")]

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/92/Moundville_Archaeological_Site_Alabama.jpg/220px-Moundville_Archaeological_Site_Alabama.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Moundville_Archaeological_Site_Alabama.jpg)

The [Moundville Archaeological Site](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moundville_Archaeological_Site "Moundville Archaeological Site") in Hale County. It was occupied by Native Americans of the [Mississippian culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mississippian_culture "Mississippian culture") from 1000 to 1450 CE.

[Indigenous peoples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_peoples "Indigenous peoples") of varying cultures lived in the area for thousands of years before the advent of European colonization. Trade with the northeastern tribes by the [Ohio River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ohio_River "Ohio River") began during the Burial Mound Period (1000 BC – 700 AD) and continued until [European contact](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_colonization_of_the_Americas "European colonization of the Americas").[\[28\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-NewYorkTimesAlmanac-29)

The agrarian [Mississippian culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mississippian_culture "Mississippian culture") covered most of the state from 1000 to 1600 AD, with one of its major centers built at what is now the [Moundville Archaeological Site](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moundville_Archaeological_Site "Moundville Archaeological Site") in [Moundville, Alabama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moundville,_Alabama "Moundville, Alabama").[\[29\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-30)[\[30\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-31) This is the second-largest complex of the classic Middle Mississippian era, after [Cahokia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cahokia "Cahokia") in present-day [Illinois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illinois "Illinois"), which was the center of the culture. Analysis of [artifacts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Artifact_\(archaeology\) "Artifact (archaeology)") from [archaeological](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archaeological "Archaeological") excavations at Moundville were the basis of scholars' formulating the characteristics of the [Southeastern Ceremonial Complex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southeastern_Ceremonial_Complex "Southeastern Ceremonial Complex") (SECC).[\[31\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-32) Contrary to popular belief, the SECC appears to have no direct links to [Mesoamerican](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mesoamerica "Mesoamerica") culture but developed independently. The Ceremonial Complex represents a major component of the religion of the Mississippian peoples; it is one of the primary means by which their religion is understood.[\[32\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-33)

Among the historical tribes of Native American people living in present-day Alabama at the time of European contact were the [Cherokee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cherokee "Cherokee"), an [Iroquoian language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iroquoian_languages "Iroquoian languages") people; and the [Muskogean-speaking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muscogee_language "Muscogee language") Alabama (*Alibamu*), [Chickasaw](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chickasaw "Chickasaw"), [Choctaw](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Choctaw "Choctaw"), Creek, and [Koasati](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coushatta "Coushatta").[\[33\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:4-34) While part of the same large language family, the [Muskogee tribes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muscogee "Muscogee") developed distinct cultures and languages.

### European settlement

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alabama\&action=edit\&section=4 "Edit section: European settlement")]

Main articles: [New France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_France "New France"), [Louisiana (New France)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisiana_\(New_France\) "Louisiana (New France)"), [French and Indian War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_and_Indian_War "French and Indian War"), [Treaty of Paris (1763)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Paris_\(1763\) "Treaty of Paris (1763)"), [New Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Spain "New Spain"), [Louisiana (New Spain)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisiana_\(New_Spain\) "Louisiana (New Spain)"), [West Florida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Florida "West Florida"), [Indian Reserve (1763)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Reserve_\(1763\) "Indian Reserve (1763)"), [American Revolutionary War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Revolutionary_War "American Revolutionary War"), [Treaty of Paris (1783)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Paris_\(1783\) "Treaty of Paris (1783)"), [Spanish West Florida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_West_Florida "Spanish West Florida"), [Seminole Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seminole_Wars "Seminole Wars"), [Adams–Onís Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adams%E2%80%93On%C3%ADs_Treaty "Adams–Onís Treaty"), [Republic of West Florida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_West_Florida "Republic of West Florida"), and [Mississippi Territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mississippi_Territory "Mississippi Territory")

The Spanish were the first Europeans to reach Alabama during their exploration of North America in the 16th century. The expedition of Hernando de Soto passed through [Mabila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mabila "Mabila") and other parts of the state in 1540. More than 160 years later, the French founded the region's first European settlement at [Old Mobile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Mobile_Site "Old Mobile Site") in 1702.[\[34\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-US50-35) The city was moved to the current site of Mobile in 1711. This area was claimed by the French from 1702 to 1763 as part of [La Louisiane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisiana_\(New_France\) "Louisiana (New France)").[\[35\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-alahisttmln-36)

After the French lost to the British in the [Seven Years' War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seven_Years%27_War "Seven Years' War"), it became part of British [West Florida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Florida "West Florida") from 1763 to 1783. After the U.S. victory in the [American Revolutionary War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Revolutionary_War "American Revolutionary War"), the territory was divided between the United States and Spain. The latter retained control of this western territory from 1783 until the surrender of the Spanish garrison at Mobile to U.S. forces on April 13, 1813.[\[35\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-alahisttmln-36)[\[36\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-annexed1-37)

Thomas Bassett, a [loyalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loyalist_\(American_Revolution\) "Loyalist (American Revolution)") to the [British monarchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Empire "British Empire") during the Revolutionary era, was one of the earliest white settlers in the state outside Mobile. He settled in the [Tombigbee District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tombigbee_District "Tombigbee District") during the early 1770s.[\[37\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-38) The district's boundaries were roughly limited to the area within a few miles of the [Tombigbee River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tombigbee_River "Tombigbee River") and included portions of what is today southern [Clarke County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clarke_County,_Alabama "Clarke County, Alabama"), northernmost [Mobile County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mobile_County,_Alabama "Mobile County, Alabama"), and most of [Washington County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_County,_Alabama "Washington County, Alabama").[\[38\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-oldsw-39)[\[39\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-colonial_mobile-40)

What are now [Baldwin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baldwin_County,_Alabama "Baldwin County, Alabama") and Mobile counties became part of [Spanish West Florida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_West_Florida "Spanish West Florida") in 1783, part of the independent [Republic of West Florida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_West_Florida "Republic of West Florida") in 1810, and finally part of the [Mississippi Territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mississippi_Territory "Mississippi Territory") in 1812. Most of what is now the northern two-thirds of Alabama was known as the [Yazoo lands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yazoo_lands "Yazoo lands") beginning during the British colonial period. It was claimed by the [Province of Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Province_of_Georgia "Province of Georgia") from 1767 onwards. Following the American Revolutionary War, it remained a part of Georgia, although heavily disputed.[\[40\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-41)[\[41\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-pickett-42)

With the exception of the area around Mobile and the Yazoo lands, what is now the lower one-third of Alabama was made part of the Mississippi Territory when it was organized in 1798. The Yazoo lands were added to the territory in 1804, following the [Yazoo land scandal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yazoo_land_scandal "Yazoo land scandal").[\[41\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-pickett-42)[\[42\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-43) Spain kept a claim on its former Spanish West Florida territory in what would become the coastal counties until the [Adams–Onís Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adams%E2%80%93On%C3%ADs_Treaty "Adams–Onís Treaty") officially ceded it to the U.S. in 1819.[\[36\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-annexed1-37)

### 19th century

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alabama\&action=edit\&section=5 "Edit section: 19th century")]

Main articles: [Organic act § List of organic acts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organic_act#List_of_organic_acts "Organic act"), [Alabama Territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alabama_Territory "Alabama Territory"), [Admission to the Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Admission_to_the_Union "Admission to the Union"), and [List of U.S. states by date of admission to the Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_U.S._states_by_date_of_admission_to_the_Union "List of U.S. states by date of admission to the Union")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/77/Mississippiterritory.PNG/200px-Mississippiterritory.PNG)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Mississippiterritory.PNG)

Mississippi territory

Before [Mississippi's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mississippi "Mississippi") admission to statehood on December 10, 1817, the more sparsely settled eastern half of the territory was separated and named the [Alabama Territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alabama_Territory "Alabama Territory"). The [United States Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Congress "United States Congress") created the Alabama Territory on March 3, 1817. [St. Stephens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Stephens,_Alabama "St. Stephens, Alabama"), now abandoned, served as the territorial capital from 1817 to 1819.[\[43\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-eoaststephens-44)

Alabama was admitted as the 22nd state on December 14, 1819, with Congress selecting [Huntsville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Huntsville,_Alabama "Huntsville, Alabama") as the site for the first [Constitutional Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constituent_assembly "Constituent assembly"). From July 5 to August 2, 1819, delegates met to prepare the new state constitution. Huntsville served as temporary capital from 1819 to 1820, when the seat of government moved to [Cahaba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cahaba,_Alabama "Cahaba, Alabama") in [Dallas County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dallas_County,_Alabama "Dallas County, Alabama").[\[44\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-45)

[Cahaba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cahaba,_Alabama "Cahaba, Alabama"), now a [ghost town](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghost_town "Ghost town"), was the first permanent state capital from 1820 to 1825.[\[45\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cahaw-46) The [Alabama Fever](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alabama_Fever "Alabama Fever") land rush was underway when the state was admitted to the Union, with settlers and land speculators pouring into the state to take advantage of fertile land suitable for cotton cultivation.[\[46\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-fever-47)[\[47\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-adahtalafvr-48) Part of the frontier in the 1820s and 1830s, its constitution provided for universal suffrage for white men.[\[48\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-SSpaces-49)

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2f/Thornhill_01.jpg/220px-Thornhill_01.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Thornhill_01.jpg)

The main house, built in 1833, at [Thornhill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thornhill_\(Forkland,_Alabama\) "Thornhill (Forkland, Alabama)") in Greene County. It is a former [Black Belt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Belt_\(region_of_Alabama\) "Black Belt (region of Alabama)") plantation.

Southeastern planters and traders from the [Upper South](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upper_South "Upper South") brought [slaves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_slavery_in_Alabama "History of slavery in Alabama") with them as the cotton [plantations in Alabama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_plantations_in_Alabama "List of plantations in Alabama") expanded. The economy of the central [Black Belt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Belt_\(region_of_Alabama\) "Black Belt (region of Alabama)") (named for its dark, productive soil) was built around large cotton [plantations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plantation_complexes_in_the_Southern_United_States "Plantation complexes in the Southern United States") whose owners' wealth grew mainly from slave labor.[\[48\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-SSpaces-49) The area also drew many poor, disenfranchised people who became [subsistence farmers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subsistence_agriculture "Subsistence agriculture"). Alabama had an estimated population of under 10,000 people in 1810, but it increased to more than 300,000 people by 1830.[\[46\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-fever-47) Most Native American tribes were [completely removed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_removal "Indian removal") from the state within a few years of the passage of the [Indian Removal Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Removal_Act "Indian Removal Act") by Congress in 1830.[\[49\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-ala-50)

From 1826 to 1846, [Tuscaloosa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tuscaloosa,_Alabama "Tuscaloosa, Alabama") served as Alabama's capital. On January 30, 1846, the Alabama legislature announced it had voted to move the capital city from Tuscaloosa to Montgomery. The first legislative session in the new capital met in December 1847.[\[50\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-capitols-51) A new capitol building was erected under the direction of [Stephen Decatur Button](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stephen_Decatur_Button "Stephen Decatur Button") of [Philadelphia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphia "Philadelphia"). The first structure burned down in 1849, but was rebuilt on the same site in 1851. This second capitol building in Montgomery remains to the present day. It was designed by Barachias Holt of [Exeter, Maine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exeter,_Maine "Exeter, Maine").[\[51\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-alcatalog-52)[\[52\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-alarchitecture-53)

#### Civil War and Reconstruction

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alabama\&action=edit\&section=6 "Edit section: Civil War and Reconstruction")]

Main articles: [Ordinance of Secession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ordinance_of_Secession "Ordinance of Secession"), [Confederate States of America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confederate_States_of_America "Confederate States of America"), and [Alabama in the American Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alabama_in_the_American_Civil_War "Alabama in the American Civil War")

By 1860, the population had increased to 964,201 people, of which nearly half, 435,080, were enslaved African Americans, and 2,690 were [free people of color](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_people_of_color "Free people of color").[\[53\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-adahtmln-54) On January 11, 1861, Alabama declared its [secession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secession_in_the_United_States "Secession in the United States") from the [Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_\(American_Civil_War\) "Union (American Civil War)"). After remaining an independent republic for a few days, it joined the [Confederate States of America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confederate_States_of_America "Confederate States of America"). The Confederacy's capital was initially at Montgomery. Alabama was heavily [involved in the American Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alabama_in_the_American_Civil_War "Alabama in the American Civil War"). Although comparatively few battles were fought in the state, Alabama contributed about 120,000 soldiers to the war effort.

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b8/Huntsville_Courthouse_Square_1864.jpg/220px-Huntsville_Courthouse_Square_1864.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Huntsville_Courthouse_Square_1864.jpg)

[Union Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_Army "Union Army") troops occupying Courthouse Square in Huntsville, following its capture and occupation by federal forces in 1864

A company of cavalry soldiers from Huntsville, Alabama, joined [Nathan Bedford Forrest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nathan_Bedford_Forrest "Nathan Bedford Forrest")'s battalion in [Hopkinsville, Kentucky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hopkinsville,_Kentucky "Hopkinsville, Kentucky"). The company wore new uniforms with yellow trim on the sleeves, collar and coattails. This led to them being greeted with "Yellowhammer", and the name later was applied to all Alabama troops in the Confederate Army.[\[54\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-55)

Alabama's slaves were freed by the 13th Amendment in 1865.[\[55\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-HistDocs-56) Alabama was under military rule from the end of the war in May 1865 until its official restoration to the Union in 1868. From 1867 to 1874, with most white citizens barred temporarily from voting and freedmen enfranchised, many African Americans emerged as political leaders in the state. Alabama was represented in Congress during this period by three African-American congressmen: [Jeremiah Haralson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeremiah_Haralson "Jeremiah Haralson"), [Benjamin S. Turner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benjamin_S._Turner "Benjamin S. Turner"), and [James T. Rapier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_T._Rapier "James T. Rapier").[\[56\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-alrecnstrctn-57)

Following the war, the state remained chiefly agricultural, with an economy tied to cotton. During the Reconstruction era, state legislators ratified a [new state constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Alabama "Constitution of Alabama") in 1868 which created the state's first public school system and expanded women's rights. Legislators funded numerous public road and railroad projects, although these were plagued with allegations of fraud and [misappropriation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Misappropriation "Misappropriation").[\[56\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-alrecnstrctn-57) Organized [insurgent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Insurgency "Insurgency"), resistance groups tried to suppress the freedmen and Republicans. These groups included The [Ku Klux Klan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ku_Klux_Klan "Ku Klux Klan"), the Pale Faces, [Knights of the White Camellia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Knights_of_the_White_Camelia "Knights of the White Camelia"), [Red Shirts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Shirts_\(United_States\) "Red Shirts (United States)"), and the [White League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_League "White League").[\[56\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-alrecnstrctn-57)

Reconstruction in Alabama ended in 1874, when the Democrats regained control of the legislature and governor's office through an election dominated by fraud and violence. They wrote another constitution in 1875,[\[56\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-alrecnstrctn-57) and the legislature passed the [Blaine Amendment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blaine_Amendment "Blaine Amendment"), prohibiting public money from being used to finance religious-affiliated schools.[\[57\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-58) The same year, legislation was approved that called for [racially segregated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racial_segregation "Racial segregation") schools.[\[58\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-jimcrowala-59) Railroad passenger cars were segregated in 1891.[\[58\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-jimcrowala-59)

### 20th century

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alabama\&action=edit\&section=7 "Edit section: 20th century")]

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/73/Birmingham_Alabama_skyline_1915.jpg/220px-Birmingham_Alabama_skyline_1915.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Birmingham_Alabama_skyline_1915.jpg)

The developing skyline of Birmingham, 1915

The new 1901 constitution of Alabama included provisions for [voter registration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voter_registration "Voter registration") that effectively [disenfranchised](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disfranchisement_after_the_Reconstruction_era "Disfranchisement after the Reconstruction era") large portions of the population, including nearly all African Americans and Native Americans, and tens of thousands of poor European Americans, through making voter registration difficult, requiring a [poll tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poll_taxes_in_the_United_States "Poll taxes in the United States") and [literacy test](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Literacy_test "Literacy test").[\[59\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-60) The 1901 constitution required racial segregation of public schools. By 1903 only 2,980 African Americans were registered in Alabama, although at least 74,000 were [literate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Literacy "Literacy"). This compared to more than 181,000 African Americans eligible to vote in 1900. The numbers dropped even more in later decades.[\[60\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-epzzsd-61) The state legislature passed additional racial segregation laws related to public facilities into the 1950s: jails were segregated in 1911; hospitals in 1915; toilets, hotels, and restaurants in 1928; and bus stop waiting rooms in 1945.[\[58\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-jimcrowala-59)

While the planter class had persuaded poor whites to vote for this legislative effort to suppress black voting, the new restrictions resulted in their disenfranchisement as well, due mostly to the imposition of a cumulative poll tax.[\[60\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-epzzsd-61) By 1941, whites constituted a slight majority of those disenfranchised by these laws: 600,000 whites vs. 520,000 African Americans.[\[60\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-epzzsd-61) Nearly all blacks had lost the ability to vote. Despite numerous legal challenges which succeeded in overturning certain provisions, the state legislature would create new ones to maintain disenfranchisement. The exclusion of blacks from the political system persisted until after passage of federal civil rights legislation in 1965 to enforce their constitutional rights as citizens.[\[61\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-62)

The rural-dominated Alabama legislature consistently underfunded schools and services for the disenfranchised African Americans, but it did not relieve them of paying taxes.[\[48\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-SSpaces-49) Partially as a response to chronic underfunding of education for African Americans in the South, the [Rosenwald Fund](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rosenwald_Fund "Rosenwald Fund") began funding the construction of what came to be known as [Rosenwald Schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rosenwald_School "Rosenwald School"). In Alabama, these schools were designed, and the construction partially financed with Rosenwald funds, which paid one-third of the construction costs. The fund required the local community and state to raise matching funds to pay the rest. Black residents effectively taxed themselves twice, by raising additional monies to supply matching funds for such schools, which were built in many rural areas. They often donated land and labor as well.[\[62\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-rosenwaldal-63)

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/be/Mount_Sinai_School_Autauga_County_July_2011_1.jpg/220px-Mount_Sinai_School_Autauga_County_July_2011_1.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Mount_Sinai_School_Autauga_County_July_2011_1.jpg)

The former [Mount Sinai School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Sinai_School "Mount Sinai School") in rural Autauga County, completed in 1919. It was one of the 387 [Rosenwald Schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rosenwald_Schools "Rosenwald Schools") built in the state.

Beginning in 1913, the first 80 Rosenwald Schools were built in Alabama for African American children. A total of 387 schools, seven teachers' houses, and several vocational buildings were completed by 1937 in the state. Several of the [surviving school buildings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Rosenwald_School_Building_Fund_and_Associated_Buildings_Multiple_Property_Submission "The Rosenwald School Building Fund and Associated Buildings Multiple Property Submission") in the state are now listed on the [National Register of Historic Places](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Register_of_Historic_Places "National Register of Historic Places").[\[62\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-rosenwaldal-63)

Continued racial discrimination and [lynchings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching "Lynching"), agricultural depression, and the failure of the cotton crops due to [boll weevil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boll_weevil "Boll weevil") infestation led tens of thousands of African Americans from rural Alabama and other states to seek opportunities in northern and midwestern cities during the early decades of the 20th century as part of the [Great Migration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Migration_\(African_American\) "Great Migration (African American)") out of the South.[\[63\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-64)[\[64\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-65) Reflecting this emigration, the population growth rate in Alabama (see "historical populations" table below) dropped by nearly half from 1910 to 1920.[\[65\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-census_data-66)

At the same time, many rural people migrated to the city of Birmingham to work in new industrial jobs. Birmingham experienced such rapid growth it was called the "Magic City".[\[66\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-67) By 1920, Birmingham was the 36th-largest city in the United States.[\[67\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-68) Heavy industry and mining were the basis of its economy. Its residents were under-represented for decades in the state legislature, which refused to redistrict after each decennial census according to population changes, as it was required by the state constitution. This did not change until the late 1960s following a lawsuit and court order.[\[68\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:0-69)

> Beginning in the 1940s, when the courts started taking the first steps to recognize the voting rights of black voters, the Alabama legislature took several counter-steps designed to disfranchise black voters. The legislature passed, and the voters ratified \[as these were mostly white voters], a state constitutional amendment that gave local registrars greater latitude to disqualify voter registration applicants. Black citizens in Mobile successfully challenged this amendment as a violation of the [Fifteenth Amendment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fifteenth_Amendment_to_the_United_States_Constitution "Fifteenth Amendment to the United States Constitution"). The legislature also changed the boundaries of [Tuskegee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tuskegee,_Alabama "Tuskegee, Alabama") to a 28-sided figure designed to fence out blacks from the city limits. The Supreme Court unanimously held that this racial "[gerrymandering](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gerrymandering "Gerrymandering")" violated the Constitution. In 1961, ... the Alabama legislature also intentionally diluted the effect of the black vote by instituting numbered place requirements for local elections.[\[69\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-vra-70)

Industrial development related to the demands of World War II brought a level of prosperity to the state not seen since before the civil war.[\[48\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-SSpaces-49) Rural workers poured into the largest cities in the state for better jobs and a higher standard of living. One example of this massive influx of workers occurred in Mobile. Between 1940 and 1943, more than 89,000 people moved into the city to work for war-related industries.[\[70\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-thomason2-71) Cotton and other [cash crops](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cash_crop "Cash crop") faded in importance as the state developed a manufacturing and service base.

Despite massive population changes in the state from 1901 to 1961, the rural-dominated legislature refused to reapportion House and Senate seats based on population, as required by the state constitution to follow the results of decennial censuses. They held on to old representation to maintain political and economic power in agricultural areas. One result was that [Jefferson County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jefferson_County,_Alabama "Jefferson County, Alabama"), containing Birmingham's industrial and economic powerhouse, contributed more than one-third of all tax revenue to the state, but did not receive a proportional amount in services. Urban interests were consistently underrepresented in the legislature. A 1960 study noted that because of rural domination, "a minority of about 25% of the total state population is in majority control of the Alabama legislature."[\[68\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:0-69)[\[71\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-pjhwpa-72)

In the United States Supreme Court cases of *[Baker v. Carr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baker_v._Carr "Baker v. Carr")* (1962) and *[Reynolds v. Sims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reynolds_v._Sims "Reynolds v. Sims")* (1964), the court ruled that the principle of "[one man, one vote](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/One_man,_one_vote "One man, one vote")" needed to be the basis of both houses of state legislatures, and that their districts had to be based on population rather than geographic counties.[\[72\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-73)[\[73\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-74)

African Americans continued to press in the 1950s and 1960s to end disenfranchisement and segregation in the state through the civil rights movement, including legal challenges. In 1954, the U.S. Supreme Court ruled in *[Brown v. Board of Education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brown_v._Board_of_Education "Brown v. Board of Education")* that public schools had to be [desegregated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Desegregation_in_the_United_States "Desegregation in the United States"), but Alabama was slow to comply. During the 1960s, under Governor [George Wallace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Wallace "George Wallace"), Alabama resisted compliance with federal demands for desegregation.[\[74\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-75)[\[75\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-76) The civil rights movement had notable events in Alabama, including the [Montgomery bus boycott](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montgomery_bus_boycott "Montgomery bus boycott") (1955–1956), [Freedom Rides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_Rides "Freedom Rides") in 1961, and 1965 Selma to Montgomery marches.[\[76\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-77) These contributed to Congressional passage and enactment of the [Civil Rights Act of 1964](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_Rights_Act_of_1964 "Civil Rights Act of 1964") and [Voting Rights Act of 1965](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voting_Rights_Act_of_1965 "Voting Rights Act of 1965") by the U.S. Congress.[\[77\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-cra64-78)[\[78\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-79)

Legal segregation ended in the states in 1964, but Jim Crow customs often continued until specifically challenged in court.[\[79\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-USDOJ-80) According to *[The New York Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_New_York_Times "The New York Times")*, by 2017, many of Alabama's African Americans were living in Alabama's cities such as Birmingham and Montgomery. Also, the Black Belt region across central Alabama "is home to largely poor counties that are predominantly African-American. These counties include Dallas, [Lowndes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lowndes_County,_Alabama "Lowndes County, Alabama"), [Marengo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marengo_County,_Alabama "Marengo County, Alabama") and [Perry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perry_County,_Alabama "Perry County, Alabama")."[\[80\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-NYT_2017-81)

In 1972, for the first time since 1901, the legislature completed the congressional redistricting based on the decennial census. This benefited the urban areas that had developed, as well as all in the population who had been underrepresented for more than sixty years.[\[71\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-pjhwpa-72) Other changes were made to implement representative state house and senate districts.

Alabama has made some changes since the late 20th century and has used new types of voting to increase representation. In the 1980s, an omnibus redistricting case, *[Dillard v. Crenshaw County](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Dillard_v._Crenshaw_County\&action=edit\&redlink=1 "Dillard v. Crenshaw County (page does not exist)")*, challenged the [at-large](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/At-large "At-large") voting for representative seats of 180 Alabama jurisdictions, including counties and school boards. At-large voting had diluted the votes of any minority in a county, as the majority tended to take all seats. Despite African Americans making up a significant minority in the state, they had been unable to elect any representatives in most of the at-large jurisdictions.[\[69\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-vra-70)

As part of settlement of this case, five Alabama cities and counties, including [Chilton County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chilton_County,_Alabama "Chilton County, Alabama"), adopted a system of [cumulative voting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proportional_representation "Proportional representation") for election of representatives in multi-seat jurisdictions. This has resulted in more proportional representation for voters. In another form of proportional representation, 23 jurisdictions use [limited voting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Limited_voting "Limited voting"), as in [Conecuh County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conecuh_County,_Alabama "Conecuh County, Alabama"). In 1982, limited voting was first tested in Conecuh County. Together use of these systems has increased the number of African Americans and women being elected to local offices, resulting in governments that are more representative of their citizens.[\[81\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-cum-82)

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c5/MSFC_Aerial_2017.jpg/220px-MSFC_Aerial_2017.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:MSFC_Aerial_2017.jpg)

George C. Marshall Space Flight Center, 2017

Beginning in the 1960s, the state's economy shifted away from its traditional lumber, steel, and textile industries because of increased foreign competition. Steel jobs, for instance, declined from 46,314 in 1950 to 14,185 in 2011.[\[82\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-83) However, the state, particularly Huntsville, benefited from the opening of the [George C. Marshall Space Flight Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marshall_Space_Flight_Center "Marshall Space Flight Center") in 1960, a major facility in the development of the Saturn rocket program and the space shuttle. Technology and manufacturing industries, such as automobile assembly, replaced some the state's older industries in the late twentieth century, but the state's economy and growth lagged behind other states in the area, such as Georgia and Florida.[\[83\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-84)

### 21st century

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alabama\&action=edit\&section=8 "Edit section: 21st century")]

In 2001, Alabama Supreme Court chief justice [Roy Moore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roy_Moore "Roy Moore") installed a statue of the [Ten Commandments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ten_Commandments "Ten Commandments") in the capitol in Montgomery. In 2002, the 11th U.S. Circuit Court ordered the statue removed, but Moore refused to follow the court order, which led to protests around the capitol in favor of keeping the monument. The monument was removed in August 2003.[\[84\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-85)

A few natural disasters have occurred in the state in the twenty-first century. In 2004, [Hurricane Ivan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hurricane_Ivan "Hurricane Ivan"), a category 3 storm upon landfall, struck the state and caused over $18 billion of damage. It was among the most destructive storms to strike the state in its modern history.[\[85\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-86) A [super outbreak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011_Super_Outbreak "2011 Super Outbreak") of 62 tornadoes hit the state in April 2011 and killed 238 people, devastating many communities.[\[86\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-87)

## Geography

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alabama\&action=edit\&section=9 "Edit section: Geography")]

Main article: [Geography of Alabama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_Alabama "Geography of Alabama")

See also: [List of Alabama counties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Alabama_counties "List of Alabama counties") and [Geology of Alabama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geology_of_Alabama "Geology of Alabama")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/87/Map_of_Alabama_terrain_NA.jpg/220px-Map_of_Alabama_terrain_NA.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Map_of_Alabama_terrain_NA.jpg)

Map of Alabama from the [National Atlas of the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Atlas_of_the_United_States "National Atlas of the United States") (2007)

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a3/OnoIslandAl.jpg/220px-OnoIslandAl.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:OnoIslandAl.jpg)

[Ono Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ono_Island,_Alabama "Ono Island, Alabama") in Baldwin County

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/85/MonteSanoStateParkhsval.jpg/220px-MonteSanoStateParkhsval.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:MonteSanoStateParkhsval.jpg)

[Monte Sano State Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monte_Sano_State_Park "Monte Sano State Park") in Huntsville

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b0/Cathedral_Cavern_2019.png/220px-Cathedral_Cavern_2019.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Cathedral_Cavern_2019.png)

[Cathedral Caverns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cathedral_Caverns_State_Park "Cathedral Caverns State Park") in [Marshall County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marshall_County,_Alabama "Marshall County, Alabama")

Alabama is the thirtieth-largest state in the United States with 52,419 square miles (135,760 km2) of total area: 3.2% of the area is water, making Alabama 23rd in the amount of surface water, also giving it the second-largest inland waterway system in the United States.[\[87\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-88) About three-fifths of the land area is part of the [Gulf Coastal Plain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_Coastal_Plain "Gulf Coastal Plain"), a gentle plain with a general descent towards the [Mississippi River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mississippi_River "Mississippi River") and the Gulf of Mexico. The [North Alabama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Alabama "North Alabama") region is mostly mountainous, with the [Tennessee River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tennessee_River "Tennessee River") cutting a large valley and creating numerous creeks, streams, rivers, mountains, and lakes.[\[88\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-NetState-89)

Alabama is bordered by the states of Tennessee to the north, Georgia to the east, Florida to the south, and Mississippi to the west. Alabama has coastline at the Gulf of Mexico, in the extreme southern edge of the state.[\[88\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-NetState-89) The state ranges in elevation from sea level[\[89\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-usgs-90) at Mobile Bay to more than 2,000 feet (610 m) in the [northeast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sand_Mountain_\(Alabama\) "Sand Mountain (Alabama)"), to [Mount Cheaha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cheaha_Mountain "Cheaha Mountain")[\[88\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-NetState-89) at 2,413 ft (735 m).[\[90\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-ngs-91)

Alabama's land consists of 22 million acres (89,000 km2) of forest or 67% of the state's total land area.[\[91\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-92) Suburban [Baldwin County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baldwin_County,_Alabama "Baldwin County, Alabama"), along the Gulf Coast, is the largest county in the state in both land area and water area.[\[92\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-93)

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/ba/NaturalBridgeAl.jpg/220px-NaturalBridgeAl.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:NaturalBridgeAl.jpg)

The Natural Bridge Rock in [Winston County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Winston_County,_Alabama "Winston County, Alabama") is the longest natural bridge east of the Rockies.

Areas in Alabama administered by the [National Park Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Park_Service "National Park Service") include [Horseshoe Bend National Military Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Horseshoe_Bend_National_Military_Park "Horseshoe Bend National Military Park") near [Alexander City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_City,_Alabama "Alexander City, Alabama"); [Little River Canyon National Preserve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Little_River_Canyon_National_Preserve "Little River Canyon National Preserve") near [Fort Payne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fort_Payne,_Alabama "Fort Payne, Alabama"); [Russell Cave National Monument](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russell_Cave_National_Monument "Russell Cave National Monument") in [Bridgeport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bridgeport,_Alabama "Bridgeport, Alabama"); [Tuskegee Airmen National Historic Site](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tuskegee_Airmen_National_Historic_Site "Tuskegee Airmen National Historic Site") in Tuskegee; and [Tuskegee Institute National Historic Site](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tuskegee_Institute_National_Historic_Site "Tuskegee Institute National Historic Site") near Tuskegee.[\[93\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-94) Additionally, Alabama has four [National Forests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_National_Forest "United States National Forest"): [Conecuh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conecuh_National_Forest "Conecuh National Forest"), [Talladega](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Talladega_National_Forest "Talladega National Forest"), [Tuskegee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tuskegee_National_Forest "Tuskegee National Forest"), and [William B. Bankhead](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_B._Bankhead_National_Forest "William B. Bankhead National Forest").[\[94\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-95) Alabama also contains the [Natchez Trace Parkway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natchez_Trace_Parkway "Natchez Trace Parkway"), the [Selma To Montgomery National Historic Trail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Selma_To_Montgomery_National_Historic_Trail "Selma To Montgomery National Historic Trail"), and the [Trail of Tears National Historic Trail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trail_of_Tears "Trail of Tears").

Natural wonders include the ["Natural Bridge"](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_Bridge,_Alabama "Natural Bridge, Alabama") rock, the longest [natural bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_arch "Natural arch") east of the [Rockies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rocky_Mountains "Rocky Mountains"), just south of [Haleyville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haleyville,_Alabama "Haleyville, Alabama"); [Cathedral Caverns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cathedral_Caverns_State_Park "Cathedral Caverns State Park"), in [Marshall County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marshall_County,_Alabama "Marshall County, Alabama"), named for its cathedral-like appearance, which features one of the largest cave entrances and one of the largest stalagmites in the world; Ecor Rouge, in [Fairhope](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fairhope,_Alabama "Fairhope, Alabama"), the highest coastline point between [Maine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maine "Maine") and Mexico;[\[95\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-96) [DeSoto Caverns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DeSoto_Caverns "DeSoto Caverns"), in [Childersburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Childersburg,_Alabama "Childersburg, Alabama"), the first officially recorded cave in the United States;[\[96\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-97) [Noccalula Falls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Noccalula_Falls_Park "Noccalula Falls Park"), in [Gadsden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gadsden,_Alabama "Gadsden, Alabama"), which has a 90-foot waterfall; [Dismals Canyon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dismals_Canyon "Dismals Canyon"), near [Phil Campbell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phil_Campbell,_Alabama "Phil Campbell, Alabama"), which is home to two waterfalls and six natural bridges and is said to have been a hideout of [Jesse James](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jesse_James "Jesse James");[\[97\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-98) Stephens Gap Cave, in [Jackson County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jackson_County,_Alabama "Jackson County, Alabama"), which has a 143-foot pit and two waterfalls and is one of the most photographed wild cave scenes in America;[\[98\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-99) Little River Canyon, near Fort Payne, one of the nation's longest mountaintop rivers; [Rickwood Caverns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rickwood_Caverns_State_Park "Rickwood Caverns State Park"), near [Warrior](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warrior,_Alabama "Warrior, Alabama"), which has an underground pool, blind cave-fish, and 260-million-year-old limestone formations; and the [Walls of Jericho](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Walls_of_Jericho_\(canyon\) "The Walls of Jericho (canyon)") canyon, on the Alabama–Tennessee border.

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c4/Cliffs_inside_the_Wetumpka_impact_crater_in_Wetumpka%2C_Alabama.jpg/220px-Cliffs_inside_the_Wetumpka_impact_crater_in_Wetumpka%2C_Alabama.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Cliffs_inside_the_Wetumpka_impact_crater_in_Wetumpka,_Alabama.jpg)

Cliffs at the rim of the [Wetumpka meteorite crater](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wetumpka_crater "Wetumpka crater")

A 5-mile (8 km)-wide meteorite impact crater is located in [Elmore County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elmore_County,_Alabama "Elmore County, Alabama"), just north of Montgomery. This is the [Wetumpka crater](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wetumpka_crater "Wetumpka crater"), the site of "Alabama's greatest natural disaster". A 1,000-foot (300 m)-wide meteorite hit the area about 80 million years ago.[\[99\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-mlvguh-100) The hills just east of downtown [Wetumpka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wetumpka,_Alabama "Wetumpka, Alabama") showcase the eroded remains of the impact crater that was blasted into the bedrock, with the area labeled the Wetumpka crater or astrobleme ("star-wound") because of the concentric rings of fractures and zones of shattered rock that can be found beneath the surface.[\[100\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-101) In 2002, Christian Koeberl with the Institute of Geochemistry University of Vienna published evidence and established the site as the 157th recognized impact crater on Earth.[\[101\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-102)

### Climate

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alabama\&action=edit\&section=10 "Edit section: Climate")]

Main article: [Climate of Alabama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_of_Alabama "Climate of Alabama")

The state is classified as [humid subtropical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humid_subtropical_climate "Humid subtropical climate") (*Cfa*) under the [Köppen climate classification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification "Köppen climate classification").[\[102\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-103) The average annual temperature is 64 °F (18 °C). Temperatures tend to be warmer in the southern part of the state with its proximity to the [Gulf of Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_of_Mexico "Gulf of Mexico"), while the northern parts of the state, especially in the [Appalachian Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Appalachian_Mountains "Appalachian Mountains") in the northeast, tend to be slightly cooler.[\[103\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-cprgsw-104) Generally, Alabama has very hot summers and mild winters with copious precipitation throughout the year. Alabama receives an average of 56 inches (1,400 mm) of rainfall annually and enjoys a lengthy growing season of up to 300 days in the southern part of the state.[\[103\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-cprgsw-104)

Summers in Alabama are among the hottest in the U.S., with high temperatures averaging over 90 °F (32 °C) throughout the summer in some parts of the state. Alabama is also prone to [tropical storms and hurricanes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tropical_cyclone "Tropical cyclone"). Areas of the state far away from the Gulf are not immune to the effects of the storms, which often dump tremendous amounts of rain as they move inland and weaken.

South Alabama reports many [thunderstorms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thunderstorm "Thunderstorm"). The Gulf Coast, around Mobile Bay, averages between 70 and 80 days per year with thunder reported. This activity decreases somewhat further north in the state, but even the far north of the state reports thunder on about 60 days per year. Occasionally, thunderstorms are severe with frequent [lightning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lightning "Lightning") and large [hail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hail "Hail"); the central and northern parts of the state are most vulnerable to this type of storm. Alabama ranks ninth in the number of deaths from lightning and tenth in the number of deaths from lightning strikes per capita.[\[104\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-105)

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ee/Phil_Campbell_tornado_damage.jpg/220px-Phil_Campbell_tornado_damage.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Phil_Campbell_tornado_damage.jpg)

Tornado damage in [Phil Campbell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phil_Campbell,_Alabama "Phil Campbell, Alabama") following the statewide [April 27, 2011, tornado outbreak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011_Super_Outbreak "2011 Super Outbreak")

Alabama, along with [Oklahoma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oklahoma "Oklahoma") and [Iowa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iowa "Iowa"), has the most confirmed [F5](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fujita_scale "Fujita scale") and [EF5](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enhanced_Fujita_scale "Enhanced Fujita scale") tornadoes of any state, according to statistics from the [National Climatic Data Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Climatic_Data_Center "National Climatic Data Center") for the period January 1, 1950, to June 2013.[\[105\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-106) Several long-tracked F5/EF5 tornadoes have contributed to Alabama reporting more tornado fatalities since 1950 than any other state. The state was affected by the [1974 Super Outbreak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1974_Super_Outbreak "1974 Super Outbreak") and was devastated tremendously by the 2011 Super Outbreak. The 2011 Super Outbreak produced a record amount of tornadoes in the state. The tally reached 62.[\[106\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-107)

The peak season for tornadoes varies from the northern to southern parts of the state. Alabama is one of the few places in the world that has a secondary tornado season in November and December besides the typically severe spring. The northern part—along the Tennessee River Valley—is most vulnerable. The area of Alabama and Mississippi most affected by tornadoes is sometimes referred to as [Dixie Alley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dixie_Alley "Dixie Alley"), as distinct from the [Tornado Alley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tornado_Alley "Tornado Alley") of the Southern Plains.

Winters are generally mild in Alabama, as they are throughout most of the Southeastern United States, with average January low temperatures around 40 °F (4 °C) in Mobile and around 32 °F (0 °C) in Birmingham. Although snow is a rare event in much of Alabama, areas of the state north of Montgomery may receive a dusting of snow a few times every winter, with an occasional moderately heavy snowfall every few years. Historic snowfall events include [New Year's Eve 1963 snowstorm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Year%27s_Eve_1963_snowstorm "New Year's Eve 1963 snowstorm") and the [1993 Storm of the Century](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1993_Storm_of_the_Century "1993 Storm of the Century"). The annual average snowfall for the Birmingham area is 2 inches (51 mm) per year. In the southern Gulf coast, snowfall is less frequent, sometimes going several years without any snowfall.

Alabama's highest temperature of 112 °F (44 °C) was recorded on September 5, 1925, in the unincorporated community of [Centerville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centerville,_Alabama "Centerville, Alabama"). The record low of −27 °F (−33 °C) occurred on January 30, 1966, in [New Market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Market,_Alabama "New Market, Alabama").[\[107\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-108)

### Flora and fauna

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alabama\&action=edit\&section=11 "Edit section: Flora and fauna")]

Main articles: [List of amphibians of Alabama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_amphibians_of_Alabama "List of amphibians of Alabama"), [List of mammals of Alabama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_mammals_of_Alabama "List of mammals of Alabama"), [List of reptiles of Alabama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_reptiles_of_Alabama "List of reptiles of Alabama"), and [Trees of Alabama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alabama_Champion_Tree_Program "Alabama Champion Tree Program")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ee/CahabaRiverNWR1.jpg/220px-CahabaRiverNWR1.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:CahabaRiverNWR1.jpg)

A stand of [Cahaba lilies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hymenocallis_coronaria "Hymenocallis coronaria") (*Hymenocallis coronaria*) in the [Cahaba River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cahaba_River "Cahaba River"), within the [Cahaba River National Wildlife Refuge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cahaba_River_National_Wildlife_Refuge "Cahaba River National Wildlife Refuge")

Alabama is home to a diverse array of [flora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flora "Flora") and [fauna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fauna "Fauna") in habitats that range from the Tennessee Valley, [Appalachian Plateau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Appalachian_Plateau "Appalachian Plateau"), and [Ridge-and-Valley Appalachians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ridge-and-Valley_Appalachians "Ridge-and-Valley Appalachians") of the north to the [Piedmont](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Piedmont_\(United_States\) "Piedmont (United States)"), [Canebrake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canebrake_\(region_of_Alabama\) "Canebrake (region of Alabama)"), and Black Belt of the central region to the [Gulf Coastal Plain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_Coastal_Plain "Gulf Coastal Plain") and beaches along the Gulf of Mexico in the south. The state is usually ranked among the top in nation for its range of overall [biodiversity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biodiversity "Biodiversity").[\[112\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-alawildlife-113)[\[113\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-outalawildlife-114)

Alabama is in the subtropical coniferous forest biome and once boasted huge expanses of pine forest, which still form the largest proportion of forests in the state.[\[112\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-alawildlife-113) It currently ranks fifth in the nation for the diversity of its flora. It is home to nearly 4,000 [pteridophyte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pteridophyte "Pteridophyte") and [spermatophyte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spermatophyte "Spermatophyte") plant species.[\[114\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-115)

[Indigenous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_species "Native species") animal species in the state include 62 [mammal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mammal "Mammal") [species](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Species "Species"),[\[115\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-outalamam-116) 93 reptile species,[\[116\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-outalarep-117) 73 [amphibian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amphibian "Amphibian") species,[\[117\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-outalaamphi-118) roughly 307 native [freshwater fish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freshwater_fish "Freshwater fish") species,[\[112\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-alawildlife-113) and 420 bird species that spend at least part of their year within the state.[\[118\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-outalabird-119) Invertebrates include 97 [crayfish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crayfish "Crayfish") species and 383 [mollusk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mollusk "Mollusk") species. 113 of these mollusk species have never been collected outside the state.[\[119\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-outalamollusk-120)[\[120\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-outalacray-121)

### Major cities

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alabama\&action=edit\&section=12 "Edit section: Major cities")]

See also: [List of metropolitan areas of Alabama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_metropolitan_areas_of_Alabama "List of metropolitan areas of Alabama") and [List of municipalities in Alabama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_municipalities_in_Alabama "List of municipalities in Alabama")

As of 2010, the state contains 461 municipalities, consisting of 174 cities and 287 towns. Covering only 9.6% of Alabama's land mass, its municipalities are home to 60.4% of its population. [Montgomery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montgomery,_Alabama "Montgomery, Alabama"), the state's capital, is the third-most populous settlement in Alabama; and the most populous city is [Huntsville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Huntsville,_Alabama "Huntsville, Alabama"). According to the U.S. Census Bureau, the state contains 12 metropolitan statistical areas. Alabama's largest metropolitan area was [Greater Birmingham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birmingham_metropolitan_area,_Alabama "Birmingham metropolitan area, Alabama").
