### Politics

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alabama\&action=edit\&section=32 "Edit section: Politics")]

During Reconstruction following the American Civil War, Alabama was occupied by federal troops of the [Third Military District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_Military_District "Third Military District") under [General John Pope](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Pope_\(military_officer\) "John Pope (military officer)"). In 1874, the political coalition of white Democrats known as the [Redeemers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Redeemers "Redeemers") took control of the state government from the Republicans, in part by suppressing the black vote through violence, fraud, and intimidation. After 1890, a coalition of White Democratic politicians passed laws to segregate and disenfranchise African American residents, a process completed in provisions of the 1901 constitution. Provisions which disenfranchised blacks resulted in excluding many poor Whites. By 1941 more Whites than Blacks had been disenfranchised: 600,000 to 520,000. The total effects were greater on the black community, as almost all its citizens were disfranchised and relegated to separate and unequal treatment under the law.

From 1901 through the 1960s, the state did not redraw election districts as population grew and shifted within the state during urbanization and industrialization of certain areas. As counties were the basis of election districts, the result was a rural minority that dominated state politics through nearly three-quarters of the century, until a series of federal court cases required redistricting in 1972 to meet equal representation. Alabama state politics gained nationwide and international attention in the 1950s and 1960s during the civil rights movement, when whites bureaucratically, and at times violently, resisted protests for electoral and social reform. Governor George Wallace, the state's only four-term governor, was a controversial figure who vowed to maintain segregation. Only after passage of the federal Civil Rights Act of 1964[\[77\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-cra64-78) and Voting Rights Act of 1965 did African Americans regain the ability to exercise suffrage, among other civil rights. In many jurisdictions, they continued to be excluded from representation by at-large electoral systems, which allowed the majority of the population to dominate elections. Some changes at the county level have occurred following court challenges to establish [single-member districts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Single-member_district "Single-member district") that enable a more diverse representation among county boards.

In 2007, the Alabama Legislature passed, and Republican governor [Bob Riley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bob_Riley "Bob Riley") signed a resolution expressing "profound regret" over slavery and its lingering impact. In a symbolic ceremony, the bill was signed in the [Alabama State Capitol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alabama_State_Capitol "Alabama State Capitol"), which housed Congress of the Confederate States of America.[\[243\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-246) In 2010, Republicans won control of both houses of the legislature for the first time in 136 years.[\[244\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-247)

As of February 2023[\[update\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alabama\&action=edit), there are a total of 3,707,233 registered voters, with 3,318,679 active, and the others inactive in the state.[\[245\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-248)

The 2023 *American Values Atlas* by [Public Religion Research Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_Religion_Research_Institute "Public Religion Research Institute") found that a majority of Alabama residents support [same-sex marriage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Same-sex_marriage_in_the_United_States "Same-sex marriage in the United States").[\[246\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-249)

### Elections

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alabama\&action=edit\&section=33 "Edit section: Elections")]

Main article: [Elections in Alabama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elections_in_Alabama "Elections in Alabama")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/53/Doug_Jones_Biden_Event.jpg/220px-Doug_Jones_Biden_Event.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Doug_Jones_Biden_Event.jpg)

Senator [Doug Jones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doug_Jones_\(politician\) "Doug Jones (politician)") won a [special election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_United_States_Senate_special_election_in_Alabama "2017 United States Senate special election in Alabama") in 2017.

#### State elections

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alabama\&action=edit\&section=34 "Edit section: State elections")]

With the disfranchisement of Blacks in 1901, the state became part of the "[Solid South](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solid_South "Solid South")", a system in which the [Democratic Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Party_\(United_States\) "Democratic Party (United States)") operated as effectively the only viable political party in every Southern state. For nearly a hundred years local and state elections in Alabama were decided in the Democratic Party [primary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partisan_primary "Partisan primary"), with generally only token [Republican](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republican_Party_\(United_States\) "Republican Party (United States)") challengers running in the general election. Since the mid- to late 20th century, however, white conservatives started shifting to the Republican Party. In Alabama, majority-white districts are now expected to regularly elect Republican candidates to federal, state and local office.

Members of the nine seats on the [Supreme Court of Alabama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Court_of_Alabama "Supreme Court of Alabama")[\[247\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-250) and all ten seats on the state appellate courts are elected to office. Until 1994, no Republicans held any of the court seats. In that general election, the then-incumbent chief justice, [Ernest C. Hornsby](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ernest_C._Hornsby "Ernest C. Hornsby"), refused to leave office after losing the election by approximately 3,000 votes to Republican [Perry O. Hooper Sr.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perry_Hooper_Sr. "Perry Hooper Sr.")[\[248\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-251) Hornsby sued Alabama and defiantly remained in office for nearly a year before finally giving up the seat after losing in court.[\[249\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-252) The Democrats lost the last of the nineteen court seats in August 2011 with the resignation of the last Democrat on the bench.

In the early 21st century, Republicans hold all seven of the statewide elected executive branch offices. Republicans hold six of the eight elected seats on the [Alabama State Board of Education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alabama_State_Board_of_Education "Alabama State Board of Education"). In 2010, Republicans took large majorities of both chambers of the state legislature, giving them control of that body for the first time in 136 years. The last remaining statewide Democrat, who served on the Alabama Public Service Commission, was defeated in 2012.[\[250\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-253)[\[251\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-254)[\[252\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-255)

Only three Republican lieutenant governors have been elected since the end of Reconstruction, when Republicans generally represented Reconstruction government, including the newly emancipated freedmen who had gained the franchise. The three GOP lieutenant governors are [Steve Windom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steve_Windom "Steve Windom") (1999–2003), [Kay Ivey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kay_Ivey "Kay Ivey") (2011–2017), and [Will Ainsworth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Will_Ainsworth "Will Ainsworth") (2019–present).

#### Local elections

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alabama\&action=edit\&section=35 "Edit section: Local elections")]

Many local offices (county commissioners, boards of education, tax assessors, mayors, etc.) in the state are still held by Democrats.[\[253\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-256)[\[254\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-257) Many metropolitan and suburban counties have voters who are majority Democrats, resulting in local elections being decided in the Democratic primary. Similarly most rural counties are majority-Republican and elections are effectively decided in the Republican Primary. However, since local governments in Alabama are weaker than in other parts of the country, Republicans have the upper hand in government.

Alabama's 67 county sheriffs are elected in partisan, at-large races, and Republicans retain the vast majority of those posts. The current split is 18 [Democrats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alabama_Democratic_Party "Alabama Democratic Party"), and 49 [Republicans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alabama_Republican_Party "Alabama Republican Party") as of 2023. However, most of the Democratic sheriffs preside over urban and more populated counties. The majority of Republican sheriffs have been elected in the more rural counties with lower population. The state of Alabama has and 11 African-American sheriffs.[\[255\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-258)

#### Federal elections

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alabama\&action=edit\&section=36 "Edit section: Federal elections")]

The state's two [U.S. senators](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Senate "United States Senate") are [Katie Britt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Katie_Britt "Katie Britt") and [Tommy Tuberville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tommy_Tuberville "Tommy Tuberville"), both of whom are Republican. In the [U.S. House of Representatives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_House_of_Representatives "United States House of Representatives"), the state is represented by seven members, five of whom are Republicans ([Mike Rogers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mike_Rogers_\(Alabama_politician\) "Mike Rogers (Alabama politician)"), [Robert Aderholt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Aderholt "Robert Aderholt"), [Dale Strong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dale_Strong "Dale Strong"), [Barry Moore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barry_Moore_\(American_politician\) "Barry Moore (American politician)"), and [Gary Palmer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gary_Palmer_\(politician\) "Gary Palmer (politician)")) and two Democrats ([Terri Sewell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terri_Sewell "Terri Sewell") and [Shomari Figures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shomari_Figures "Shomari Figures")).

## Education

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alabama\&action=edit\&section=37 "Edit section: Education")]

Main article: [Education in Alabama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_in_Alabama "Education in Alabama")

### Primary and secondary education

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alabama\&action=edit\&section=38 "Edit section: Primary and secondary education")]

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a8/Vestavia_Hills_High_School.jpg/220px-Vestavia_Hills_High_School.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Vestavia_Hills_High_School.jpg)

[Vestavia Hills High School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vestavia_Hills_High_School "Vestavia Hills High School") in the suburbs of Birmingham

Public primary and secondary education in Alabama is under the purview of the Alabama State Board of Education as well as local oversight by 67 county school boards and 60 city boards of education. Together, 1,496 individual schools provide education for 744,637 elementary and secondary students.[\[256\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-qfacts-259)

Public school funding is appropriated through the Alabama Legislature through the Education Trust Fund. In FY 2006–2007, Alabama appropriated $3,775,163,578 for primary and secondary education. That represented an increase of $444,736,387 over the previous fiscal year. In 2007, more than 82 percent of schools made adequate yearly progress (AYP) toward student proficiency under the National [No Child Left Behind](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/No_Child_Left_Behind_Act "No Child Left Behind Act") law, using measures determined by the state of Alabama.

While Alabama's public education system has improved in recent decades, it lags behind in achievement compared to other states. According to U.S. Census data (2000), Alabama's high school graduation rate (75%) is the fourth lowest in the U.S. (after [Kentucky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kentucky "Kentucky"), [Louisiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisiana "Louisiana") and Mississippi).[\[257\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-260) The largest educational gains were among people with some college education but without degrees.[\[258\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-261) According to National Assessment of Educational Progress (NEAP), Alabama ranks 39 in reading and 40 in math among fourth-grade students in the rankings from 2022.[\[259\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-262)

Generally prohibited in the West at large, [school corporal punishment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/School_corporal_punishment "School corporal punishment") is not unusual in Alabama, with 27,260 public school students [paddled](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paddle_\(spanking\) "Paddle (spanking)") at least one time, according to government data for the 2011–2012 school year.[\[c\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-263)[\[260\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-CORPUN_US_SCHOOL-264) The rate of school corporal punishment in Alabama is surpassed by only Mississippi and Arkansas.[\[260\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-CORPUN_US_SCHOOL-264)

### Colleges and universities

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alabama\&action=edit\&section=39 "Edit section: Colleges and universities")]

Main article: [List of colleges and universities in Alabama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_colleges_and_universities_in_Alabama "List of colleges and universities in Alabama")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d4/Harrison-plaza2.jpg/220px-Harrison-plaza2.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Harrison-plaza2.jpg)

Harrison Plaza at the [University of North Alabama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_North_Alabama "University of North Alabama") in Florence. The school was chartered as LaGrange College by the [Alabama Legislature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alabama_Legislature "Alabama Legislature") in 1830.

Alabama's programs of higher education include 14 four-year public universities, two-year community colleges, and 17 private, undergraduate and graduate universities. In the state are four medical schools (as of fall 2015) [UAB Heersink School of Medicine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Alabama_at_Birmingham_School_of_Medicine "University of Alabama at Birmingham School of Medicine"), University of South Alabama and [Alabama College of Osteopathic Medicine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alabama_College_of_Osteopathic_Medicine "Alabama College of Osteopathic Medicine") and The Edward Via College of Osteopathic Medicine—Auburn Campus), two veterinary colleges (Auburn University and [Tuskegee University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tuskegee_University "Tuskegee University")), a dental school ([UAB School of Dentistry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UAB_School_of_Dentistry "UAB School of Dentistry"), an optometry college (UAB School of Optometry), two pharmacy schools (Auburn University and [Samford University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samford_University "Samford University")), and five law schools ([University of Alabama School of Law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Alabama_School_of_Law "University of Alabama School of Law"), [Birmingham School of Law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birmingham_School_of_Law "Birmingham School of Law"), [Cumberland School of Law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cumberland_School_of_Law "Cumberland School of Law"), [Miles Law School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miles_Law_School "Miles Law School"), and the [Thomas Goode Jones School of Law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Goode_Jones_School_of_Law "Thomas Goode Jones School of Law")). Public, post-secondary education in Alabama is overseen by the [Alabama Commission on Higher Education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alabama_Commission_on_Higher_Education "Alabama Commission on Higher Education") and the Alabama Department of Postsecondary Education. Colleges and universities in Alabama offer degree programs from two-year associate degrees to a multitude of doctoral level programs.[\[261\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-ache-265)

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/bf/William_J._Samford_Hall.jpg/220px-William_J._Samford_Hall.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:William_J._Samford_Hall.jpg)

William J. Samford Hall at [Auburn University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Auburn_University "Auburn University")

The largest single campus is the University of Alabama, located in Tuscaloosa, with 37,665 enrolled for fall 2016.[\[262\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-266) Troy University was the largest institution in the state in 2010, with an enrollment of 29,689 students across four Alabama campuses ([Troy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Troy,_Alabama "Troy, Alabama"), [Dothan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dothan,_Alabama "Dothan, Alabama"), Montgomery, and [Phenix City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phenix_City,_Alabama "Phenix City, Alabama")), as well as sixty learning sites in seventeen other states and eleven other countries. The oldest institutions are the public [University of North Alabama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_North_Alabama "University of North Alabama") in Florence and the Catholic Church-affiliated [Spring Hill College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spring_Hill_College "Spring Hill College") in Mobile, both founded in 1830.[\[263\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-una-267)[\[264\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-shc-268)

Accreditation of academic programs is through the [Southern Association of Colleges and Schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Association_of_Colleges_and_Schools "Southern Association of Colleges and Schools") (SACS) as well as other subject-focused national and international accreditation agencies such as the [Association for Biblical Higher Education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Association_for_Biblical_Higher_Education "Association for Biblical Higher Education") (ABHE),[\[265\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-269) the [Council on Occupational Education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_on_Occupational_Education "Council on Occupational Education") (COE),[\[266\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-270) and the [Accrediting Council for Independent Colleges and Schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Accrediting_Council_for_Independent_Colleges_and_Schools "Accrediting Council for Independent Colleges and Schools") (ACICS).[\[267\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-271)

According to the 2024 *[U.S. News & World Report](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._News_%26_World_Report "U.S. News & World Report")*, Alabama had three universities ranked in the top 100 Public Schools in America ([Auburn University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Auburn_University "Auburn University") at 47, [University of Alabama at Birmingham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Alabama_at_Birmingham "University of Alabama at Birmingham") (UAB) at 76, and [University of Alabama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Alabama "University of Alabama") at 91).[\[268\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-272)

According to the 2024 *U.S. News & World Report*, Alabama had four [tier one](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/College_and_university_rankings "College and university rankings") universities (Auburn University, University of Alabama at Birmingham (UAB), [University of Alabama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Alabama "University of Alabama") and [University of Alabama in Huntsville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Alabama_in_Huntsville "University of Alabama in Huntsville")).[\[269\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-273)

### Literature

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alabama\&action=edit\&section=42 "Edit section: Literature")]

Main article: [Alabama literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alabama_literature "Alabama literature")

Alabama literature is characterized by themes of race and issues of gender and war, and is influenced by events such as the [American Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Civil_War "American Civil War"), the [Reconstruction era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reconstruction_era "Reconstruction era"), the [Civil Rights Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_Rights_Movement "Civil Rights Movement"), and the [Vietnam War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vietnam_War "Vietnam War"). Some notable examples of Alabama literature include Harper Lee's *[To Kill A Mockingbird](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/To_Kill_A_Mockingbird "To Kill A Mockingbird")*, Winston Groom's *[Forrest Gump](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forrest_Gump_\(novel\) "Forrest Gump (novel)")*, Fannie Flagg's *[Fried Green Tomatoes at the Whistlestop Cafe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fried_Green_Tomatoes_at_the_Whistlestop_Cafe "Fried Green Tomatoes at the Whistlestop Cafe")*, and the biographies of [Rosa Parks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rosa_Parks "Rosa Parks") and [Martin Luther King Jr.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martin_Luther_King_Jr. "Martin Luther King Jr.")

| [Huntsville Havoc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Huntsville_Havoc "Huntsville Havoc")                             | [Huntsville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Huntsville,_Alabama "Huntsville, Alabama") | Ice hockey                                                                      | Southern Professional Hockey League                                                                                                              | [Von Braun Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Von_Braun_Center "Von Braun Center")                                     |
| [Montgomery Biscuits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montgomery_Biscuits "Montgomery Biscuits")                    | [Montgomery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montgomery,_Alabama "Montgomery, Alabama") | Baseball                                                                        | Southern League (Double-A)                                                                                                                       | [Montgomery Riverwalk Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montgomery_Riverwalk_Stadium "Montgomery Riverwalk Stadium") |
| [Rocket City Trash Pandas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rocket_City_Trash_Pandas "Rocket City Trash Pandas")     | [Madison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madison,_Alabama "Madison, Alabama")          | Baseball                                                                        | Southern League (Double-A)                                                                                                                       | [Toyota Field](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toyota_Field_\(Madison,_Alabama\) "Toyota Field (Madison, Alabama)")         |

The [Talladega Superspeedway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Talladega_Superspeedway "Talladega Superspeedway") motorsports complex hosts a series of [NASCAR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NASCAR "NASCAR") events. It has a seating capacity of 143,000 and is the thirteenth largest stadium in the world and sixth largest stadium in America. Also, the [Barber Motorsports Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barber_Motorsports_Park "Barber Motorsports Park") has hosted [IndyCar Series](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IndyCar_Series "IndyCar Series") and [Rolex Sports Car Series](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rolex_Sports_Car_Series "Rolex Sports Car Series") races.

The [ATP Birmingham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ATP_Birmingham "ATP Birmingham") was a [World Championship Tennis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Championship_Tennis "World Championship Tennis") tournament held from 1973 to 1980.

Alabama has hosted several professional golf tournaments, such as the 1984 and 1990 [PGA Championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PGA_Championship "PGA Championship") at [Shoal Creek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shoal_Creek_Golf_and_Country_Club "Shoal Creek Golf and Country Club"), the [Barbasol Championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barbasol_Championship "Barbasol Championship") ([PGA Tour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PGA_Tour "PGA Tour")), the [Mobile LPGA Tournament of Champions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mobile_LPGA_Tournament_of_Champions "Mobile LPGA Tournament of Champions"), [Airbus LPGA Classic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Airbus_LPGA_Classic "Airbus LPGA Classic"), and [Yokohama Tire LPGA Classic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yokohama_Tire_LPGA_Classic "Yokohama Tire LPGA Classic") ([LPGA Tour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LPGA_Tour "LPGA Tour")), and [The Tradition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Tradition "The Tradition") ([Champions Tour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Champions_Tour "Champions Tour")).

#### College sports

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alabama\&action=edit\&section=45 "Edit section: College sports")]

College football is extremely popular in Alabama, particularly the University of Alabama [Crimson Tide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alabama_Crimson_Tide "Alabama Crimson Tide") and Auburn University [Tigers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Auburn_Tigers "Auburn Tigers"), rivals in the [Southeastern Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southeastern_Conference "Southeastern Conference"). Alabama averages over 100,000 fans per game and Auburn averages over 80,000—both numbers among the top twenty in the nation.[\[272\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-276) Bryant–Denny Stadium is the home of the Alabama football team, and has a [seating capacity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seating_capacity "Seating capacity") of 101,821,[\[273\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-277) and is the fifth largest stadium in America.[\[274\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-278) Jordan-Hare Stadium is the home field of the Auburn football team and seats up to 87,451.[\[275\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-279)

[Protective Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protective_Stadium "Protective Stadium") is home of the [UAB Blazers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UAB_Blazers "UAB Blazers") football program and the [Birmingham Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birmingham_Bowl "Birmingham Bowl"). It seats 45,000.[\[276\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-280) [Ladd–Peebles Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ladd%E2%80%93Peebles_Stadium "Ladd–Peebles Stadium") in Mobile is the home of the University of South Alabama football team, and serves as the home of the NCAA [Senior Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Senior_Bowl "Senior Bowl"), [LendingTree Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LendingTree_Bowl "LendingTree Bowl"), and Alabama-Mississippi All Star Classic; the stadium seats 40,646.[\[277\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-281) In 2009, Bryant–Denny Stadium and Jordan-Hare Stadium became the homes of the [Alabama High School Athletic Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alabama_High_School_Athletic_Association "Alabama High School Athletic Association") state football championship games, after previously being held at Legion Field in Birmingham.[\[278\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-282)

### Aviation

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alabama\&action=edit\&section=47 "Edit section: Aviation")]

Main article: [Aviation in Alabama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aviation_in_Alabama "Aviation in Alabama")

Major airports with sustained operations in Alabama include [Birmingham-Shuttlesworth International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birmingham-Shuttlesworth_International_Airport "Birmingham-Shuttlesworth International Airport") (BHM), [Huntsville International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Huntsville_International_Airport "Huntsville International Airport") (HSV), [Dothan Regional Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dothan_Regional_Airport "Dothan Regional Airport") (DHN), [Mobile Regional Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mobile_Regional_Airport "Mobile Regional Airport") (MOB), [Montgomery Regional Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montgomery_Regional_Airport "Montgomery Regional Airport") (MGM), [Northwest Alabama Regional Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northwest_Alabama_Regional_Airport "Northwest Alabama Regional Airport") (MSL) and [Northeast Alabama Regional Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northeast_Alabama_Regional_Airport "Northeast Alabama Regional Airport") (GAD).

### Rail

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alabama\&action=edit\&section=48 "Edit section: Rail")]

For rail transport, [Amtrak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amtrak "Amtrak") schedules the *[Crescent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crescent_\(train\) "Crescent (train)")*, a daily passenger train, running from New York to New Orleans with station stops at [Anniston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anniston_station "Anniston station"), [Birmingham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birmingham_station_\(Alabama\) "Birmingham station (Alabama)"), and [Tuscaloosa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tuscaloosa_station "Tuscaloosa station").

### Roads

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alabama\&action=edit\&section=49 "Edit section: Roads")]

Alabama has six major interstate routes: [Interstate 65](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_65_in_Alabama "Interstate 65 in Alabama") (I-65) travels north–south roughly through the middle of the state; [I-20](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_20_in_Alabama "Interstate 20 in Alabama")/[I-59](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_59_in_Alabama "Interstate 59 in Alabama") travel from the central west Mississippi state line to Birmingham, where I-59 continues to the north-east corner of the state and I-20 continues east towards [Atlanta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atlanta "Atlanta"); [I-85](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_85_in_Alabama "Interstate 85 in Alabama") originates in Montgomery and travels east-northeast to the Georgia state line, providing a main thoroughfare to Atlanta; and [I-10](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_10_in_Alabama "Interstate 10 in Alabama") traverses the southernmost portion of the state, traveling from west to east through Mobile. [I-22](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_22_in_Alabama "Interstate 22 in Alabama") enters the state from Mississippi and connects Birmingham with [Memphis, Tennessee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Memphis,_Tennessee "Memphis, Tennessee"). In addition, there are currently five auxiliary interstate routes in the state: [I-165](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_165_\(Alabama\) "Interstate 165 (Alabama)") in Mobile, [I-359](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_359 "Interstate 359") in Tuscaloosa, [I-459](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_459 "Interstate 459") around Birmingham, [I-565](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_565 "Interstate 565") in Decatur and Huntsville, and [I-759](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_759 "Interstate 759") in Gadsden. A sixth route, [I-685](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_685_\(Alabama\) "Interstate 685 (Alabama)"), will be formed when I-85 is rerouted along a new southern bypass of Montgomery. A proposed northern bypass of Birmingham will be designated as [I-422](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_422 "Interstate 422"). Since a direct connection from I-22 to I-422 will not be possible, [I-222](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_222 "Interstate 222") has been proposed, as well.

Several U.S. Highways also pass through the state, such as [U.S. Route 11](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Route_11_in_Alabama "U.S. Route 11 in Alabama") (US-11), [US-29](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Route_29_in_Alabama "U.S. Route 29 in Alabama"), [US-31](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Route_31_in_Alabama "U.S. Route 31 in Alabama"), [US-43](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Route_43_in_Alabama "U.S. Route 43 in Alabama"), [US-45](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Route_45_in_Alabama "U.S. Route 45 in Alabama"), [US-72](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Route_72_in_Alabama "U.S. Route 72 in Alabama"), [US-78](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Route_78_in_Alabama "U.S. Route 78 in Alabama"), [US-80](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Route_80_in_Alabama "U.S. Route 80 in Alabama"), [US-82](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Route_82_in_Alabama "U.S. Route 82 in Alabama"), [US-84](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Route_84_in_Alabama "U.S. Route 84 in Alabama"), [US-90](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Route_90_in_Alabama "U.S. Route 90 in Alabama"), [US-98](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Route_98_in_Alabama "U.S. Route 98 in Alabama"), [US-231](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Route_231_in_Alabama "U.S. Route 231 in Alabama"), [US-278](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Route_278_in_Alabama "U.S. Route 278 in Alabama"), [US-280](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Route_280_in_Alabama "U.S. Route 280 in Alabama"), [US-331](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Route_331_in_Alabama "U.S. Route 331 in Alabama"), [US-411](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Route_411_in_Alabama "U.S. Route 411 in Alabama"), and [US-431](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Route_431_in_Alabama "U.S. Route 431 in Alabama").

There are four toll roads in the state: Montgomery Expressway in Montgomery; Northport/Tuscaloosa Western Bypass in Tuscaloosa and [Northport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northport,_Alabama "Northport, Alabama"); Emerald Mountain Expressway in Wetumpka; and Beach Express in [Orange Beach](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orange_Beach,_Alabama "Orange Beach, Alabama").

### Ports

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alabama\&action=edit\&section=50 "Edit section: Ports")]

The [Port of Mobile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Mobile "Port of Mobile"), Alabama's only saltwater port, is a large seaport on the Gulf of Mexico with inland waterway access to the [Midwest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Midwestern_United_States "Midwestern United States") by way of the [Tennessee–Tombigbee Waterway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tennessee%E2%80%93Tombigbee_Waterway "Tennessee–Tombigbee Waterway"). The Port of Mobile was ranked 12th by tons of traffic in the United States during 2009.[\[279\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-tonstraffic-283) The newly expanded [container terminal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Container_port "Container port") at the Port of Mobile was ranked as the 25th busiest for container traffic in the nation during 2011.[\[280\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-containers-284) The state's other ports are on rivers with access to the Gulf of Mexico.

Water ports of Alabama, listed from north to south:

## Further reading

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alabama\&action=edit\&section=56 "Edit section: Further reading")]

For a detailed bibliography, see [History of Alabama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Alabama "History of Alabama").

- Atkins, Leah Rawls, Wayne Flynt, William Warren Rogers, and David Ward. *Alabama: The History of a Deep South State* (1994).
- Flynt, Wayne. *Alabama in the Twentieth Century* (2004).
- Owen Thomas M. [*History of Alabama and Dictionary of Alabama Biography*](https://archive.org/details/historyofalabama01owen) (4 vols, 1921).
- Jackson, Harvey H. *Inside Alabama: A Personal History of My State* (2004).
- Mohl, Raymond A. "Latinization in the Heart of Dixie: Hispanics in Late-twentieth-century Alabama" *[Alabama Review](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alabama_Review "Alabama Review")* (2002, 55(4): 243–274). [ISSN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISSN_\(identifier\) "ISSN (identifier)") [0002-4341](https://www.worldcat.org/search?fq=x0:jrnl\&q=n2:0002-4341)
- Peirce, Neal R. *The Deep South States of America: People, Politics, and Power in the Seven Deep South States* (1974).
- Williams, Benjamin Buford. *A Literary History of Alabama: The Nineteenth Century* (1979).
- Harvey H., Jackson III, ed. (2000) \[1941]. *The WPA Guide to 1930s Alabama*. University of Alabama Press.
